But if they’re saying it’s too low, then… I’m trying to take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about gaining weight affect your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still getting to work, still doing my shifts at the garage, and I’m playing in my basketball league, so it’s not like everything’s falling apart or anything.\n\nBut… yeah, I guess it’s made things a little tense at home. I just feel better keeping to what I’m used to, but I can see how it probably bothers them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't playing basketball?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yeah… but I don’t think basketball is the whole reason.\n\nMost days I just kind of keep it simple — coffee in the morning, and then maybe a small sandwich later, or half of whatever’s around. With the shop running late and the house being busy, it feels easier to stick to my own little routine. I know that probably doesn’t sound like a lot, but it just feels manageable that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat as little as you do now if you weren't playing basketball?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. I mean, basketball might make me think about it a little more, but even without it I’d probably still keep things pretty light.\n\nMost days I just do coffee in the morning, and then maybe a small sandwich later, or just half of whatever’s around. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult, it just feels easier with the shop running late and things busy at home. I kind of have my own simple rules, I guess, and sticking to that makes me feel better… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m, like, terrified of it… but yeah, I guess there’s some fear there.\n\nIf I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my mind kind of goes right to, “I’m getting soft.” And then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around the cars at work, or up and down the court. I know that might sound kind of overblown, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel good about your body if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and maybe it depends on how much, but honestly I don’t think I’d feel great about it.\n\nEven just a pound on the scale or my jeans feeling tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. And then I worry I’d be slower moving around cars at work or up and down the court. I know that probably sounds a little dramatic, but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling better when you're playing basketball and working?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d like to say it’s more about feeling better for work and basketball… that sounds better, maybe. But if I’m being honest, it’s kind of tied to avoiding weight gain too.\n\nLike, if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. And then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. So… yeah, I think the weight part is in there more than I probably want to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… so I’m here mostly because of a work physical. The doctor there said my weight was too low and thought I should come talk to someone.\n\nI mean, I don’t really feel bad or anything. Honestly, I kind of feel lighter on my feet when I play basketball, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down moving around. But… they seemed concerned, so I figured I should follow up like they said.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m, like, terrified of it… but yeah, I guess it bothers me more than maybe it should. If I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Like I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or up and down the court playing basketball. \n\nThat probably sounds kind of dumb, but… yeah, it gets in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever limit what you eat or avoid certain foods?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think of it as avoiding foods exactly. It’s more like I’ve got a routine that keeps things simple, if that makes sense.\n\nMost mornings I’ll just have coffee, and then later maybe a small sandwich or, you know, half of whatever’s around. With the shop running late and the house being busy, I just feel better sticking to my own rules about it. It doesn’t feel like a big thing to me, but… I guess maybe it sounds a little limited when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, a little. Not like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but if I eat more than usual I start wondering if it’s going to show, you know?\n\nLike if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. And then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. I know that probably sounds kind of vain or maybe not the right way to think about it… but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, maybe. I didn’t really think of it as significant, but the work physical is what kind of got me sent here. The doctor there said my weight was too low.\n\nTo me, I don’t know… I’ve just felt lighter, like quicker on my feet playing basketball, and not as weighed down moving around the garage. So I didn’t see it as a bad thing, exactly. But if they’re saying it’s too low, then… I’m trying to take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about gaining weight affect your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still getting to work, still doing my shifts at the garage, and I’m playing in my basketball league, so it’s not like everything’s falling apart or anything.\n\nBut… yeah, I guess it’s made things a little tense at home. I don’t always sit down for regular dinners with everybody, and that turns into questions — like if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with them. I don’t mean to make it a whole thing. I just feel better keeping to what I’m used to, but I can see how it probably bothers them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't playing basketball?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yeah… but I don’t think basketball is the whole reason.\n\nMost days I just kind of keep it simple — coffee in the morning, and then maybe a small sandwich later, or half of whatever’s around. With the shop running late and the house being busy, it feels easier to stick to my own little routine. I know that probably doesn’t sound like a lot, but it just feels manageable that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat as little as you do now if you weren't playing basketball?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. I mean, basketball might make me think about it a little more, but even without it I’d probably still keep things pretty light.\n\nMost days I just do coffee in the morning, and then maybe a small sandwich later, or just half of whatever’s around. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult, it just feels easier with the shop running late and things busy at home. I kind of have my own simple rules, I guess, and sticking to that makes me feel better… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m, like, terrified of it… but yeah, I guess there’s some fear there.\n\nIf I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my mind kind of goes right to, “I’m getting soft.” And then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around the cars at work, or up and down the court. I know that might sound kind of overblown, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel good about your body if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and maybe it depends on how much, but honestly I don’t think I’d feel great about it.\n\nEven just a pound on the scale or my jeans feeling tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. And then I worry I’d be slower moving around cars at work or up and down the court. I know that probably sounds a little dramatic, but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling better when you're playing basketball and working?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d like to say it’s more about feeling better for work and basketball… that sounds better, maybe. But if I’m being honest, it’s kind of tied to avoiding weight gain too.\n\nLike, if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. And then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. Then I get worried I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. I mean… not most of the time, exactly. I’m still getting to work at the garage, and I’m still playing in my basketball league, so it’s not like I’ve stopped everything.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think it’s almost every waking moment. That sounds stronger than I’d say it is.

It’s more like it gets triggered. If I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, then I start thinking I’m getting soft, and then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or up and down the court. But I wouldn’t want to say it’s every second of the day, if that makes sense.

If I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Like I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or up and down the court playing basketball. \n\nThat probably sounds kind of dumb, but… yeah, it gets in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever limit what you eat or avoid certain foods?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think of it as avoiding foods exactly. It’s more like I’ve got a routine that keeps things simple, if that makes sense.\n\nMost mornings I’ll just have coffee, and then later maybe a small sandwich or, you know, half of whatever’s around. With the shop running late and the house being busy, I just feel better sticking to my own rules about it. It doesn’t feel like a big thing to me, but… I guess maybe it sounds a little limited when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, a little. Not like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but if I eat more than usual I start wondering if it’s going to show, you know?\n\nLike if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. I didn’t really think of it as significant, but the work physical is what kind of got me sent here. So I didn’t see it as a bad thing, exactly. But if they’re saying it’s too low, then… I’m trying to take that seriously.\n\nDoctor: Does your concern about gaining weight affect your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still getting to work, still doing my shifts at the garage, and I’m playing in my basketball league, so it’s not like everything’s falling apart or anything.\n\nBut… yeah, I guess it’s made things a little tense at home. I don’t always sit down for regular dinners with everybody, and that turns into questions — like if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with them. I don’t mean to make it a whole thing. I just feel better keeping to what I’m used to, but I can see how it probably bothers them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't playing basketball?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yeah… but I don’t think basketball is the whole reason.\n\nMost days I just kind of keep it simple — coffee in the morning, and then maybe a small sandwich later, or half of whatever’s around. With the shop running late and the house being busy, it feels easier to stick to my own little routine. I know that probably doesn’t sound like a lot, but it just feels manageable that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to eat as little as you do now if you weren't playing basketball?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yeah. I mean, basketball might make me think about it a little more, but even without it I’d probably still keep things pretty light.\n\nMost days I just do coffee in the morning, and then maybe a small sandwich later, or just half of whatever’s around. It’s not like I’m trying to be difficult, it just feels easier with the shop running late and things busy at home. I kind of have my own simple rules, I guess, and sticking to that makes me feel better… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m, like, terrified of it… but yeah, I guess there’s some fear there.\n\nIf I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my mind kind of goes right to, “I’m getting soft.” And then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around the cars at work, or up and down the court. I know that might sound kind of overblown, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel good about your body if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and maybe it depends on how much, but honestly I don’t think I’d feel great about it.\n\nEven just a pound on the scale or my jeans feeling tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. And then I worry I’d be slower moving around cars at work or up and down the court. I know that probably sounds a little dramatic, but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling better when you're playing basketball and working?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d like to say it’s more about feeling better for work and basketball… that sounds better, maybe. But if I’m being honest, it’s kind of tied to avoiding weight gain too.\n\nLike, if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. And then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. So… yeah, I think the weight part is in there more than I probably want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all about that, but if I’m being honest, avoiding gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I get worried I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. I know that might not sound like a lot, but it gets in my head.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding weight gain the main reason you limit what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t really like admitting that, because I know it probably sounds vain or not the “right” reason.\n\nBut when I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or up and down the court. So… yeah, avoiding weight gain is probably the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of gaining weight is overwhelming or dominates your thoughts most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say overwhelming all the time… I mean, I can still do what I need to do.\n\nBut yeah, it gets pretty loud in my head. If I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. So… maybe it does take up more space than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat as little as you do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that fear is probably what keeps me eating as little as I do.\n\nLike, if the scale bumps up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or up and down the court. I know that might not make total sense, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably every day, if I’m being honest. Not nonstop, but it shows up.\n\nLike if I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I’m worrying I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. I know that maybe sounds kind of… I don’t know, silly, but yeah, it’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight interfere with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: I mean… not most of the time, exactly. I’m still getting to work at the garage, and I’m still playing in my basketball league, so it’s not like I’ve stopped everything.\n\nBut… I guess it does affect things at home. So… yeah, I think the weight part is in there more than I probably want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all about that, but if I’m being honest, avoiding gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I get worried I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. I know that might not sound like a lot, but it gets in my head.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding weight gain the main reason you limit what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t really like admitting that, because I know it probably sounds vain or not the “right” reason.\n\nBut when I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or up and down the court. So… yeah, avoiding weight gain is probably the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of gaining weight is overwhelming or dominates your thoughts most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say overwhelming all the time… I mean, I can still do what I need to do.\n\nBut yeah, it gets pretty loud in my head. If I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. So… maybe it does take up more space than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat as little as you do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that fear is probably what keeps me eating as little as I do.\n\nLike, if the scale bumps up even a pound or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or up and down the court. I know that might not make total sense, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably every day, if I’m being honest. Not nonstop, but it shows up.\n\nLike if I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Then I’m worrying I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or running up and down the court. I know that maybe sounds kind of… I don’t know, silly, but yeah, it’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight interfere with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: I mean… not most of the time, exactly. I’m still getting to work at the garage, and I’m still playing in my basketball league, so it’s not like I’ve stopped everything.\n\nBut… I guess it does affect things at home. I tend to avoid sitting down for regular family meals, and that’s caused some tension. People keep asking me if I’m okay, which… I usually say I’m fine, but I know they’re noticing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight something that you think about almost every waking moment?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s almost every waking moment. That sounds stronger than I’d say it is.\n\nIt’s more like it gets triggered. If I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, then I start thinking I’m getting soft, and then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work or up and down the court. But I wouldn’t want to say it’s every second of the day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight something that you think about throughout the entire day?\n\nPatient: Not throughout the entire day, no… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more when something sets it off. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, then I start thinking I’m getting soft. And then I worry I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or up and down the court. But it’s not like I’m thinking about it every minute, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight so intense that it feels unbearable at times?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say unbearable, exactly… I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt can get pretty uncomfortable, though. Like if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my head goes right to, “I’m getting soft.” Then I start worrying I’ll be slower at work moving around cars, or slower running up and down the court. But… unbearable might be too strong? I’m not sure if that’s the right way to put it."
